```text
BDDL files exist on the server. The issue is that `get_tasks_by_perturbation` uses `benchmark.get_benchmark_dict()['libero_spatial']()` which only returns the 10 nominal tasks. The perturbation task names from `task_classification.json` never match those 10 task names, so it returns 0.

The problem: the `benchmark` module from the standard LIBERO library doesn't know about the LIBERO-Plus perturbation variants. The LIBERO-Plus fork at `/root/LIBERO-plus/` has the BDDL files but might not register them as benchmark tasks.


I need to refactor `get_tasks_by_perturbation` to bypass the benchmark registry for perturbation tasks and instead directly parse the BDDL files and init_states from the LIBERO-Plus directories, using `task_classification.json` to map tasks to their perturbation categories. Let me first check whether LIBERO-Plus registered itself as a separate benchmark that might already include these variants.

Good - the LIBERO-Plus fork is installed as a package and the paths point to the correct directories within the fork. The BDDL files for perturbation variants exist.

The problem is clear: `benchmark.get_benchmark_dict()['libero_spatial']()` only returns 10 task names (the nominal tasks), but the `task_classification.json` has perturbation task names (with `_table_1`, `_add_1`, etc. suffixes). The `get_tasks_by_perturbation` function tries to match against the benchmark's 10 task names, so it always returns 0 matches for perturbation types.


I need to rethink the approach — instead of relying on the benchmark registry which only knows about nominal tasks, I should load the perturbation task names directly from the task classification file and match those against the actual BDDL files in the LIBERO-Plus directory. Looking at how `run_episode` actually uses task indices to load the BDDL files directly, rather than relying on the benchmark's nominal task names. This should let me bypass the mismatch between the full task names in task_classification.json and the limited set returned by `get_task_names()`.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I understand the full flow. The issue is in `get_tasks_by_perturbation`:

For non-nominal perturbation types:
1. It gets `target_names` from `task_classification.json` - e.g. all names with category "Background Textures"
2. It then calls `bm.get_task_names()` from the standard benchmark which only has 10 nominal names
3. The intersection is always empty because perturbed names never match nominal names

For the `create_libero_env` function:
- If `selected_idx < 0`: it's a nominal task (handles by looking up BDDL file directly)


- If `selected_idx >= 0`: it uses the benchmark to get the task

The fix is to change `get_tasks_by_perturbation` so that for perturbation types, it doesn't rely on the benchmark's task list. Instead, it should return the perturbation task names directly (or special indices that `create_libero_env` can handle). Looking at how `create_libero_env` handles nominal tasks with negative indices to look up BDDL files directly...

Examining the code structure...

I could use the same negative index pattern for perturbation tasks, mapping them to their BDDL files directly, or I could change `get_tasks_by_perturbation` to return task names instead of indices and have `create_libero_env` use those names to locate the files.

The cleanest approach is to extend the existing negative index pattern: I'll cache perturbation task names at the module level, have `get_tasks_by_perturbation` return negative indices that map to those names, then modify `create_libero_env` to handle this lookup.

Building the cache and lookup logic... I need to modify `create_libero_env` to handle three cases: string indices for perturbation tasks (which need direct BDDL lookup), negative indices for nominal tasks, and non-negative indices for benchmark tasks. This is the minimal fix to make the system work.
```

```text
I see - the init_states only exist for the nominal tasks (no perturbation suffix). The perturbation variants share the same initial states as their nominal counterparts. So for a task like `pick_up_the_black_bowl_..._table_1`, the init_state comes from the base task `pick_up_the_black_bowl_...`.

This makes sense - perturbation variants modify the environment (table texture, camera angle, etc.) but start from the same initial states as the nominal task.

So the flow for perturbation tasks should be:


1. Get the perturbation task name from `task_classification.json` (e.g. `pick_up_..._table_1`)
2. Use the BDDL file at `bddl_files/libero_spatial/{name}.bddl`
3. Extract the base task name by stripping the perturbation suffix and use its init_state

I can use the existing regex pattern to identify and remove perturbation tokens from the task name.

Writing the patch script...

More importantly, if the baseline policy without LoRA also fails, then the problem isn't the LoRA at all—it's something fundamental about the setup or the model itself. Loading the policy through OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy...

So I need to fix how MetaBrain generates actions to match what OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy does—likely in how it preprocesses observations or postprocesses actions.

Build data dict in openpi format (numpy, unbatched)\\n        base_img = np.asarray(obs_dict[\\\"observation/image\\\"], dtype=np.uint8)\\n        wrist_img = np.asarray(obs_dict[\\\"observation/wrist_image\\\"], dtype=np.uint8)\\n        state_raw = np.asarray(obs_dict[\\\"observation/state\\\"], dtype=np.float32)\\n\\n        # Normalize state (8D) before model transforms pad it to 32D\\n        s_mean = self.norm_stats[\\\"state_mean\\\"][:8].cpu().numpy()\\n        s_std = self.norm_stats[\\\"state_std\\\"][:8].cpu().numpy()\\n        state_normed = (state_raw - s_mean) / (s_std + 1e-6)\\n\\n        data = {\\n            \\\"image\\\": {\\n                \\\"base_0_rgb\\\": base_img,\\n                \\\"left_wrist_0_rgb\\\": wrist_img,\\n                \\\"right_wrist_0_rgb\\\": np.zeros_like(base_img),\\n            },\\n            \\\"image_mask\\\": {\\n                \\\"base_0_rgb\\\": True,\\n                \\\"left_wrist_0_rgb\\\": True,\\n                \\\"right_wrist_0_rgb\\\": False,\\n            },\\n            \\\"state\\\": state_normed,\\n            \\\"prompt\\\": str(obs_dict.get(\\\"prompt\\\", \\\"\\\")),\\n        }\\n\\n        # Apply openpi model transforms: ResizeImages, TokenizePrompt, PadStatesAndActions\\n        for transform in self._model_transforms:\\n            data = transform(data)\\n\\n        # Convert to batched torch tensors (all values are now numeric numpy arrays)\\n        batched = jax.tree.map(\\n            lambda x: torch.from_numpy(np.asarray(x)).to(self.device)[None, ...],\\n            data,\\n        )\\n\\n        # <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I see the `infer` method. It does:
1. Gets observation, normalizes state with norm_stats
2. Applies openpi transforms
3. Runs predict_action (which merges LoRA, runs model, restores)
4. Unnormalizes actions with norm_stats
5. Applies delta_actions (adds current state[:6] to predicted action[:6])
6. Slices to 7 DOF

The warning "No norm_stats.json found, using identity normalization" followed by "Using JSON norm_stats (state=32D, action=32D, use_delta=True)" suggests the code has multiple norm stats loading paths.


I'm tracing through how norm stats actually get loaded. The `_load_pi0_checkpoint` function pulls the model weights and norm-related buffers from the safetensors file, then `_build_norm_stats` processes those buffers to construct the final normalization statistics.

The real issue might be that `eval_base_no_lora.py` uses the OpenPI Policy wrapper with a properly calibrated action pipeline, while the MetaBrain assembly has its own action normalization logic that could have a bug. But the key thing to verify first is whether MetaBrain with routing disabled even works at baseline performance on nominal tasks—if that fails too, the problem is in MetaBrain's inference pipeline itself, not the LoRA adapter.

Now I see the actual problem: `eval_base_no_lora.py` loads from `pi0_libero` checkpoint, not `pi0_base`, and uses a converted checkpoint format specifically designed for the OpenPI inference pipeline. So the two evaluation scripts are actually using different checkpoint formats and inference pipelines entirely.

Looking at `evaluate_libero_plus.py`, it does support `--policy_type openpi`, but the OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy doesn't have LoRA injection implemented yet for routing modes beyond 'none'. The real issue is deciding whether to fix the MetaBrain inference pipeline or implement LoRA support in the OpenPI policy.

Let me check if MetaBrain with `routing=none` also fails—if it does, the inference pipeline itself is broken. The eval log showed 220 steps (max limit) with a FAIL, meaning the robot is moving but not completing tasks. The MetaBrain's `infer` method converts delta actions to absolute actions, and if the scaling or delta computation is wrong, the robot could move incorrectly or too slowly.

Examining the action conversion logic...

Still writing action logic...

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Critical finding: ALL previous eval runs show 0% success rate - both `eval_none` (no LoRA) and `eval_par` runs. This confirms that the MetaBrain inference pipeline itself is broken, not just the LoRA evaluation.

The `eval_base_no_lora.py` (which uses `OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy`) achieves 60% success rate on nominal tasks. But `evaluate_libero_plus.py` using the `MetaBrain` class (which reimplements inference) gets 0% success rate.


The issue is that MetaBrain.infer() has a bug somewhere in the preprocessing, normalization, or denormalization steps. The working approach uses OpenPI's official Policy class which handles all these transforms correctly, while the reimplemented version in MetaBrain has introduced an error. Since I need to evaluate LoRA on perturbation tasks but the MetaBrain inference is broken, I'm weighing whether to debug and fix the implementation or find an alternative approach.

I'm leaning toward injecting LoRA into the OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy instead, since it already has a working inference pipeline. The strategy would be to intercept the model's forward pass and inject or remove LoRA weights at that point. Let me examine how OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy works to see if this is feasible.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I understand the full picture. The `OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy`:

1. Uses OpenPI's official Policy class with all proper transforms
2. Has a working `infer()` method that achieves 60% success rate (from eval_base_no_lora.py)
3. Currently only supports `routing_mode='none'` (no LoRA injection)
4. For other modes, it just returns dummy routing weights `[0.33, 0.33, 0.34]` without actually applying LoRA

The `MetaBrain` class has LoRA injection but its inference pipeline is broken (0% success rate even without LoRA).


I can fix this by integrating LoRA injection into `OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy` — I'll load the LoRA weight matrices from checkpoints, compute routing weights based on the mode (universal or PAR router), and inject the merged LoRA deltas before each forward pass while restoring the original weights afterward, reusing the helper methods from `MetaBrain`.

The tricky part is that OpenPI's Policy class calls the model forward internally during `policy.infer()`, so I can't easily intercept it. My best approach is to modify the model weights in-place before calling `policy.infer()` — compute the routing weights, merge the LoRA matrices with the model weights, add the deltas to the linear layers, run inference with the modified weights, then restore everything afterward.

For the routing mode, the universal case is straightforward: just inject the LoRA with weight 1.0 before each call. The PAR router is more involved since it needs hidden states from a partial forward pass through PaliGemma layer 6, which I'll tackle later. For now, I'm going to enhance the OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy class by adding LoRA loading, a factory method to initialize from a checkpoint, and the logic to inject and restore LoRA weights depending on the routing mode.

Modifying the policy class...
```

Now I have the full LoRABank class and understand how it works. The plan:

1. Add LoRA injection to `OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy`
2. The approach: load LoRA weights, inject them into model weights before `policy.infer()`, restore after
3. For "universal" routing: always use weight=1.0 for all LoRA modules (effectively same as applying the universal adapter)

But there's a subtlety: with `LoRABank`, the approach is to load 3 experts and merge their deltas with routing weights. For the universal LoRA eval, I'll load the universal adapter as a single expert and apply it directly with weight=1.0.


I'll implement this by loading a single universal LoRA adapter, computing the delta for each module as scaling times B @ A, then adding that delta to the model weights before inference and subtracting it after. I need to enhance `OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy` with a LoRABank instance, a dictionary to store the original frozen weights, and a router for PAR mode, then modify the `infer()` method to handle the injection and restoration.

Since the model is accessible as `self.model` in the Policy wrapper, I can directly modify the linear layer weights. Let me write out the updated script and cancel the current failing eval job first.

_nominal_index_to_name,\n    _PERTURB_TOKENS_RE,\n    obs_to_model_input,\n    ALL_PERTURBATION_TYPES,\n    PERTURBATION_TO_3CLASS,\n    MAX_STEPS,\n    REPLAN_STEPS,\n    NUM_STEPS_WAIT,\n The regex is finding the first occurrence of `_table` in "from_table_center" when it should be finding the last one at the end. I need to use `re.findall` or search from the right to capture the final perturbation suffix instead.

Fallback: strip from last perturbation token\n    matches = list(_PERTURB_TOKENS_RE.finditer(perturbed_name))\n    if matches:\n        result = perturbed_name[:matches[-1].start()]\n        # Try second-to-last if result doesn't look like a full task name\n        if len(matches) > 1 and len(result) < 30:\n            result = perturbed_name[:matches[0].start()]\n        _base_name_cache[perturbed_name] = result\n        return result\n    return perturbed_name'''\n\nassert old in content, \"Could not find old _extract_base_task_name\"\ncontent = content.replace(old, new)\n\nwith open(filepath, \"w\") as f:\n    f.write(content)\nprint(\"Fixed _extract_base_task_name v2\")\n\n# Verify\nimport sys\nsys.path.insert(0, '/root/meta_brain_lora_routing/phase1')\nimport importlib\nimport evaluate_libero_plus\nimportlib.reload(evaluate_libero_plus)\ntests = [\n    ('pick_up_the_black_bowl_from_table_center_and_place_it_on_the_plate_table_1',\n     'pick_up_the_black_bowl_from_table_center_and_place_it_on_the_plate'),\n    ('pick_up_the_black_bowl_between_the_plate_and_the_ramekin_and_place_it_on_the_plate_view_0_0_100_2_352_initstate_0',\n     'pick_up_the_black_bowl_between_the_plate_and_the_ramekin_and_place_it_on_the_plate'),\n    ('pick_up_the_black_bowl_on_the_stove_and_place_it_on_the_plate_add_10',\n     'pick_up_the_black_bowl_on_the_stove_and_place_it_on_the_plate'),\n    ('pick_up_the_black_bowl_in_the_top_drawer_of_the_wooden_cabinet_and_place_it_on_the_plate_table_12',\n     'pick_up_the_black_bowl_in_the_top_drawer_of_the_wooden_cabinet_and_place_it_on_the_plate'),\n]\nfor input_name, expected in tests:\n    result = evaluate_libero_plus._extract_base_task_name(input_name)\n    status = \"OK\" if result == expected else f\"FAIL (got: {result[-40:]})\"\n    print(f\"  {status}: ...{input_name[-40:]}\")\nFIXEOF\nsource /root/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh && conda activate base && python /tmp/fix_extract2.py",

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user wants to evaluate v3 LoRA experts + PAR routing on server westd-34142 (GPU cuda:3). Specifically:
   - Evaluate Universal LoRA on perturbed tasks (background_textures, objects_layout, robot_initial_states)
   - If time allows, evaluate PAR routing (3 experts + router)
   - Produce a comparison table: Frozen vs Universal vs PAR
   - Priority: Get universal LoRA eval working first, then PAR routing

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - PI0 model (3.3B params, PaliGemma + expert architecture) for robotic manipulation
   - PEFT/LoRA adapters (rank=16, alpha=32, scaling=2.0, targeting PaliGemma Q/K/V/O projections)
   - PAR (Perturbation-Adaptive Routing): 2-layer MLP classifier on PaliGemma layer 6 hidden states → 3-class routing
   - Scheme C: Dynamic LoRA weight merging (merged_A = Σ p_k·A_k, delta = scaling·merged_B@merged_A)
   - OpenPI Policy pipeline (proper transform chain for action prediction)
   - LIBERO-Plus: Perturbation variants of LIBERO tasks (background_textures, camera_viewpoints, objects_layout, robot_initial_states)
   - MetaBrain assembly vs OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy (two inference paths - MetaBrain is BROKEN, OpenPI works)

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - `/root/meta_brain_lora_routing/phase1/eval_lora_openpi.py` (NEWLY CREATED)
     - The main evaluation script that works correctly
     - Uses OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy for working inference + LoRA weight injection
     - Contains LoRAInjector class, PARRouterLite class, run_episode function
     - Key code: LoRAInjector.inject() adds delta=scaling*(B@A) to model weights before inference, restore() copies back frozen weights
   
   - `/root/meta_brain_lora_routing/phase1/evaluate_libero_plus.py` (PATCHED)
     - Fixed get_tasks_by_perturbation to return task name strings for perturbation types
     - Fixed _extract_base_task_name to use prefix matching against nominal names
     - These fixes are imported by eval_lora_openpi.py
   
   - `/root/meta_brain_lora_routing/phase1/meta_brain_assembly.py`
     - MetaBrain class with LoRA injection (Scheme C) - but its infer() pipeline is broken (0% success)
     - LoRABank class: loads PEFT adapters, computes merged deltas
     - from_checkpoints() factory: loads pi0 model + router + LoRA experts
   
   - `/root/meta_brain_lora_routing/phase1/openpi_metabrain_policy.py`
     - Working inference pipeline using OpenPI's official Policy class
     - CONVERTED_CKPT_PATH = '/root/autodl-tmp/checkpoints/pi0_libero_openpi_converted.safetensors'
     - Currently only supports routing_mode='none' natively
   
   - `/root/meta_brain_lora_routing/phase1/par_router.py`
     - PARRouterModel: nn.Sequential(Linear(2048,256), ReLU, Dropout(0.1), Linear(256,3))
     - PARRouter: wraps model, provides route() and route_argmax() methods
     - Router checkpoint: /root/meta_brain_lora_routing/checkpoints/phase1/par_router/best_router.pt

4. Errors and fixes:
   - **get_tasks_by_perturbation returning 0 perturbation tasks**:
     - Root cause: benchmark.get_benchmark_dict()['libero_spatial']() only returns 10 nominal task names; perturbation task names from task_classification.json never match
     - Fix: Changed function to return task name strings directly from task_classification.json instead of trying to match against benchmark
   
   - **_extract_base_task_name matching "table" inside task names**:
     - Root cause: regex `_(table|add|view|...)(_|\d)` matches `_table_c` in "from_table_center"
     - First fix attempt: Use last regex match instead of first - partially worked but failed for compound suffixes like `_view_..._initstate_...`
     - Final fix: Use prefix matching against known nominal task names (sorted by length descending)
   
   - **MetaBrain.infer() producing 0% success rate**:
     - Root cause: The custom inference pipeline in MetaBrain reimplements transforms incorrectly (all historical eval_none and eval_par results show 0%)
     - Fix: Created new eval_lora_openpi.py that uses the working OpenPIMetaBrainPolicy + direct weight injection for LoRA
   
   - **MuJoCo state shape mismatch errors** (ongoing, non-critical):
     - Some perturbation tasks have different DOF counts than the base task's init_states
     - These episodes are caught as exceptions and skipped in the eval loop

5. Problem Solving:
   - Discovered MetaBrain inference pipeline is fundamentally broken by checking historical output files (all show 0% success rate)
   - Solved by creating a new hybrid approach: use OpenPI's working inference pipeline + inject LoRA weight deltas directly into model linear layers before each inference call
   - The eval is now running successfully with episodes completing and achieving reasonable success rates

6. All user messages:
   - Initial task message providing detailed instructions for evaluating v3 LoRA experts + PAR routing, including checkpoint locations, eval plan, commands to run, and priority ordering
   - "CRITICAL: Respond with TEXT ONLY..." (the current summary request)

7. Pending Tasks:
   - Wait for current universal LoRA eval to complete (background_textures, objects_layout, robot_initial_states, nominal × 20 episodes each)
   - Run baseline (routing=none) eval for comparison using the same eval_lora_openpi.py script

1. First run: 12/15 = 80.0% (timestamps 04:45:xx - 04:46:01)
2. Second run: 17/20 = 85.0% (timestamps 04:46:32 - 04:48:28)

The JSON file was written at the beginning with just the first run's data showing 0/17. It looks like the JSON was being written incrementally during the first run - and the process was restarted.

Wait, that doesn't make sense either. The JSON says 0/17 but the logs show successful episodes. Let me look more carefully...


Actually, I think I see what happened. The JSON file was created at 04:42:37, but the background_textures task didn't start until 04:45:49 in the logs.
